# The Hans Clevers group — people, 1985–2026

Who worked in Hans Clevers' laboratory over the last thirty years, how many papers each person co-authored with him, and when their first joint paper appeared. Derived from the complete PubMed record set in `clevers_publications.md`, with roles established by targeted web research one person at a time.

> **On the group websites.** Both `hubrecht.eu` and `prinsesmaximacentrum.nl` refuse automated retrieval (HTTP 403), so the current member lists could not be read directly. What could be established from search-engine-indexed content is that Clevers returned to the Hubrecht Institute as *distinguished group leader* of the Clevers group, and separately leads the Clevers group (formerly the Organoid group) at the Princess Máxima Center, where the focus is pediatric cancer organoid biobanks. Individual current members named on those pages — among them Johan van Es, Harry Begthel, Jeroen Korving, Jens Puschhof, Joep Beumer and Antonella Dost — were confirmed through their own institutional profiles instead, and appear in Table A. Anyone currently in the group who has not yet co-authored an indexed paper with Clevers cannot appear in these tables at all, since the tables are built from co-authorship.

## The lab's three homes

Clevers' group has occupied three institutional addresses, which is what makes affiliation strings usable as a membership signal:

| Period | Institution as it appears in PubMed |
| --- | --- |
| 1991–2002 | Department of Immunology, University Hospital / University Medical Center Utrecht |
| 2002–2012 | Hubrecht Laboratory, Netherlands Institute for Developmental Biology (NIOB); renamed **Hubrecht Institute** (KNAW & UMC Utrecht) in 2007 |
| 2015–present | **Princess Máxima Center for Pediatric Oncology**, held jointly with the Hubrecht; Oncode Institute from 2018; from 2022 also Roche pRED / Institute of Human Biology, Basel |

Clevers was President of the KNAW (2012–2015) and Head of Pharma Research and Early Development at Roche (from March 2022), running the Utrecht group throughout.

## How to read the tables

- **Papers with Clevers** — distinct PubMed records co-authored with him.
- **First / last co-publication** — years of the earliest and latest shared paper.
- **Group-affiliation window** — the first and last year in which *that person's own affiliation line* on a shared paper names the Hubrecht, the NIOB, the Princess Máxima Center, or the Utrecht immunology department. It is a lower bound on their time in the lab, not a contract length.
- **Evidence tier A** (Table A) — group affiliation documented in PubMed. **Tier C** (Table B) — co-author on a shared paper for whom PubMed holds no affiliation at all. Tier C is *absence of data, not evidence of non-membership*: PubMed indexed only the first author's address before ~2014, so most people who passed through the lab in its first two decades land in Table B. Several of the lab's best-known alumni are there for that reason and are annotated individually.

**Totals.** 4673 distinct co-authors across 876 publications. 461 have a Hubrecht / NIOB / Máxima / Utrecht-immunology affiliation documented on at least one shared paper.


## Comprehensive summary

### What the numbers say

Across 876 PubMed-indexed papers spanning 1985–2026, Hans Clevers has **4,673 distinct
co-authors**. Of those, **461 carry an affiliation naming one of the group's three homes**
on at least one shared paper. That 461 is the defensible core of "people who worked in or
with the Clevers group"; the remaining 4,212 are a mixture of genuine pre-2014 lab members
whose addresses PubMed never recorded, and the very large external collaborator network that
comes with consortium papers, mouse-genetics collaborations and organoid biobank consortia.

Within the 461:

| | |
| --- | ---: |
| Documented at the Hubrecht Institute / NIOB | 258 |
| Documented at the Princess Máxima Center | 111 |
| Documented at the UMC Utrecht immunology department | 49 |
| Documented at more than one of these | 43 |
| With **10 or more** joint papers | 67 |
| With **5 or more** joint papers | 146 |
| With exactly one joint paper | 177 |

The distribution by decade of first documented group affiliation — 23 in the 1990s, 30 in the
2000s, 190 in the 2010s, 218 in the 2020s — is **not** a picture of the lab growing twenty-fold.
It is almost entirely an artefact of PubMed starting to index all-author affiliations around
2014. The lab was already large in the 2000s; those people are simply invisible in Table A and
sit in Table B instead.

### The permanent core

A small number of people are visible across decades and give the group its continuity. They
are the ones who show both very high paper counts and affiliation windows spanning fifteen
years or more:

- **Johan H. van Es** — 112 joint papers, first in 1997, still publishing in 2026. Senior staff
  scientist; the longest-running scientific presence in the lab after Clevers himself.
- **Marc van de Wetering** — 101 joint papers from 1991. Started as Clevers' PhD student on
  T-cell transcription factors, stayed through the Wnt years, and now runs pediatric cancer
  organoids at the Princess Máxima. His span, 1991–2025, covers essentially the whole period
  this document is about.
- **Harry Begthel** (72 papers from 2003) and **Jeroen Korving** (39 from 2003) — the group's
  histology technicians. Their names on a paper are a reliable marker that the work was done
  in-house.
- **Maaike van den Born** (35 from 2002) and **Stieneke van den Brink** — research technicians
  who carried much of the mouse and organoid culture work.
- **Wim B. M. de Lau** — 26 papers from 1992 to the 2020s, spanning immunology and the
  R-spondin/LGR5 receptor work.

### The training pipeline, and what it produced

The striking feature of the co-authorship record is how many people left the group to run their
own labs. The alumni verified in this compilation now lead groups at, among others, A*STAR
Singapore (**Nick Barker**), the Francis Crick Institute (**Vivian Li**), MPI-CBG Dresden
(**Meritxell Huch**), IBS Korea via Cambridge and IMBA Vienna (**Bon-Kyoung Koo**), IRB Barcelona
(**Eduard Batlle**), Keio University (**Toshiro Sato**), ETH Zurich (**Helmuth Gehart**),
the DKFZ (**Jens Puschhof**), Georg-Speyer-Haus Frankfurt (**Henner Farin**), the University of
Copenhagen (**Antonella Dost**), Karolinska Institutet (**Amanda Andersson-Rolf**), University
Hospital Würzburg (**Kai Kretzschmar**), TU Dresden (**Daniel Stange**), IMG Prague
(**Vladimír Kořínek**), BSRC Alexander Fleming Athens (**Pantelis Hatzis**), McGill
(**Alexander Gregorieff**), UCSF (**Jeroen Roose**), UMC Utrecht (**Hugo Snippert**,
**Onur Basak**) and the Princess Máxima itself (**Jarno Drost**, **Benedetta Artegiani**,
**Delilah Hendriks**).

Two people took the technology commercial: **Robert Vries** and **Sylvia Boj**, both Clevers
postdocs, became CEO and Chief Research Officer respectively of Hubrecht Organoid Technology
(HUB), the non-profit that licenses the organoid patents. **Else Driehuis** followed the same
route into HUB and then Xilis.

### Three eras of the group

**1991–2002, immunology.** The group sat in the Department of Immunology at the University
Hospital Utrecht and worked on HMG-box transcription factors — TCF-1, LEF-1, SOX-4 — and on
T-cell development. The people from this era are almost all in Table B, because PubMed did not
index their addresses: Mariëtte Oosterwegel, Marc Schilham, Petra Moerer, Jeroen Roose,
Vladimír Kořínek, Dennis Dooijes, Marc van Beest, Miranda Molenaar. The pivot came in 1997,
when Korinek and Barker showed that β-catenin/TCF-4 is constitutively active in APC-mutant
colon cancer — the paper that turned an immunology lab into a Wnt-and-cancer lab.

**2002–2015, the Hubrecht and Lgr5.** The lab moved to the Hubrecht Laboratory / NIOB and
found, in sequence, the Wnt/TCF intestinal signature (van der Flier), EphB-controlled cell
positioning (Batlle), Lgr5 as an adult stem-cell marker (Barker, 2007) and the intestinal
organoid culture (Sato, 2009). This is the period that produced the largest number of
independent group leaders per head.

**2015–present, organoids and pediatric oncology.** The group became a joint appointment with
the Princess Máxima Center. Work broadened to cancer organoid biobanks, CRISPR engineering in
organoids (Geurts, Andersson-Rolf), the microbiome (Puschhof, Pleguezuelos-Manzano), lung and
brain organoids (Dost; Artegiani and Hendriks), and pediatric tumour models. From 2022 Clevers
also heads Roche pRED and the Institute of Human Biology in Basel, and the most recent papers
carry Roche affiliations alongside the Utrecht ones.

### Frequent co-authors who were *not* group members

Several of the highest-count names in the co-authorship table ran their own laboratories and
should not be read as lab members. The most prominent are **Peter J. Peters** (48 joint papers;
electron microscopy, NKI then Maastricht), **Owen J. Sansom** and **Alan R. Clarke** (Apc/Myc
mouse genetics, Cardiff/Beatson), **Alexander van Oudenaarden** and **Edwin Cuppen** (fellow
Hubrecht group leaders), **Albert J. R. Heck** and **Shabaz Mohammed** (Utrecht proteomics),
**Marianne Verhaar** and **Maarten Rookmaaker** (UMC Utrecht nephrology), and **Jeffrey Beekman**
and **Cornelis van der Ent** (UMC Utrecht cystic fibrosis). They are flagged individually in the
tables where a role has been assigned.

### How far to trust this

The paper counts and first-co-publication years are exact — they are computed from the complete
PubMed record set and can be recomputed from `data/coauthors.csv`. The membership classification
is as good as PubMed's affiliation indexing allows, which means it is reliable from 2014 onward
and systematically incomplete before then. The role assignments were made one person at a time
from institutional pages, thesis-defence announcements and published biographies; they cover the
most prolific members and the alumni with a public research profile, not all 461 people. Roles
that could not be sourced to an explicit statement are marked "(inferred)", and people who
merely collaborated are marked as such rather than being left to look like lab members.
